                                                                                   Abstract

                                                  The flat DHS connection JDHS constructed in arXiv:2602.01461 is smooth on the
                                              configuration space of n points on a fixed compact Riemann surface Σ of arbitrary
                                              genus h, takes values in an infinite-dimensional Lie algebra t̂h,n and is invariant
                                              under the modular group Sp(2h, Z). This paper is the first in a series for a program
                                              whose goal is to extend the connection JDHS to a global flat connection on the
                                              Teichmüller space Th,n valued in the Lie algebra of derivations of t̂h,n . Upon the
                                              choice of local coordinates adapted to the map Th,n → Th , such a connection splits
                                              into three pieces: JDHS , a piece L corresponding to holomorphic directions of Th
                                              and a third piece corresponding to anti-holomorphic directions in Th . In this paper,
                                              we isolate the system of equations satisfied by L and obtain its solution locally and
                                              explicitly. The construction of a global extension of JDHS to Th,n and the extension
                                              of the meromorphic connection of arXiv:1112.0864 to Th,n , are relegated to future
                                              publications in this series.

                                                 4
1         Introduction
The construction of flat connections on various manifolds, with values in suitable infinite-
dimensional Lie algebras, is of considerable interest both from the viewpoints of physics
and mathematics. In physics, state-of-the-art computations in perturbative quantum
field theory and string theory benefit from systematic integration methods in terms of
polylogarithms, namely iterated integrals arising from path-ordered exponentials of such
flat connections. In mathematics, they encode information on the mixed motives which
originate from fundamental groups, and give rise to proofs of 1-formality of the latter.
    Recently, flat connections on the configuration spaces Cf n (Σ) of n points on a Rie-
mann surface Σ of genus h ≥ 1, valued in the Lie algebra t̂h,n , were studied from various
viewpoints.1 Modular invariant connections that are real analytic and single-valued on
Cf n (Σ) were produced for n = 1 in [2] and for general n in [3]. Multivalued meromorphic
connections with non-trivial automorphy conditions were introduced in [4].2 Single-valued
meromorphic variants were introduced in [7, 8]. Although these flat connections differ in
their defining properties, they are related to one another by composing a gauge transfor-
mation and a Lie algebra automorphism [7, 9], and this can be exploited to relate the
corresponding associated spaces of polylogarithms [9].
    The above-mentioned connections were all studied for a fixed complex structure on Σ.
The goal of this work and its sequels is to extend these connections by components that
account for variations in complex-structure moduli while preserving flatness. This task
decomposes as follows: to first assemble the individual flat connections into a vertical
connection relative to the fibration,
                                                 ϖ : Th,n → Th                                              (1.1)
Here, Th,n is the Teichmüller space of compact Riemann surfaces of genus h with n marked
points, Th denotes the space Th,0 , and the fibers of ϖ are isomorphic to Cf n (Σ), as rep-
resented schematically in the lower box of figure 1 (see for example [10]). The next task
is that of producing a global extension to Teichmüller space of one of the above connec-
tions, namely, the search for a flat connection on Th,n with values in the Lie algebra3 of
derivations der(t̂h,n ), whose restriction to the fibers of ϖ coincides with the image of the
initial connection under the adjoint Lie algebra morphism t̂h,n → der(t̂h,n ).
    1
    The Lie algebra t̂h,n is isomorphic to the Lie algebra of the pro-unipotent completion of the braid
group π1 (Cf n (Σ)) [1]. A definition in terms of generators and relations will be given in section 2.3.
    2
        See [5, 6, 7] for explicit representations of the differential forms in the meromorphic connection of [4].
    3
     In this paper, we shall denote by der(g) the Lie algebra of continuous derivations of a graded complete
Lie algebra g; it is therefore the graded completion of the Lie algebra of derivations of the direct sum of
its graded components. The latter algebra is supported in degree ≥ 0 if g is generated in degree 1.

of the connection dΘ − JΘ for the lower box is relegated to subsequent work.



1.1    Motivation
In genus h = 1, the global extension problem of the connections has already been studied
from different perspectives. In the multivalued meromorphic context, it was solved for
n = 1 in [11] and for arbitrary n in [12]. In the smooth single-valued context, it was
solved for n = 1 in [13].
   In genus h ≥ 2, the motivation for solving the extension problem is threefold:
    (a) Its solution should lead to the introduction of new modular functions and modular
tensors on Th , beyond the ones identified in [14, 15] at low rank and more generally in
[16, 17, 18, 19]. The solution is also expected to explain further interrelations between

                                                             6
modular tensors and the moduli variation of polylogarithms.
    (b) In topology, it is expected to give rise to an explicit flat connection on the moduli
space Mh,n such as the one constructed in section 15 of [20] for h ≥ 2, possibly giving an
alternative proof of the results of [20] on relative completions of mapping class groups.
    (c) In physics, the genus-one global connection of [12], and its component along the
direction of variations of the modulus of Σ in particular, has become a central tool used to
organize the spaces of elliptic Feynman integrals [21, 22] and genus-one string amplitudes
[23, 24] in terms of iterated integrals over modular forms. A corresponding impact is
expected to result from the solution of the higher-genus globalization problem.


1.2    The global extension problem for the DHS connection
In this paper, we shall address the extension problem in the smooth single-valued and
modular invariant context provided by the multivariable DHS connection which was in-
troduced in [3].
    To set the stage, denote by Σ a fixed smooth, compact, orientable surface without
boundary. Recall that the DHS construction assigns to a complex structure J on Σ a
smooth connection on Cf n (Σ), valued in the Lie algebra t̂h,n . Within this context, the list
of tasks outlined above decomposes as:
   (a) the construction of a t̂h,n -valued 1-form JDHS on Th,n as above;
   (b) the construction of a der(t̂h,n )-valued 1-form Jglobal over Th,n , such that
   (c) d − Jglobal is a flat connection on Th,n , and
   (d) the restriction of Jglobal to the vertical subbundle ker(T ϖ) of the fibration (1.1)
coincides with the image of JDHS by the adjoint Lie algebra morphism t̂h,n → der(t̂h,n ).
    We relegate the solution of these tasks to subsequent papers in the series of articles
for this program mentioned in the abstract.


1.3    The local (1, 0) extension problem for the DHS connection
As a first step towards a solution of (b) above, we will construct in a subsequent paper
in this series a bundle Θ∗ over Th,n of “1-forms modulo the (0, 1)-direction in the base of
(1.1)”, which will be a quotient of the bundle of 1-forms and therefore equipped with a
differential dΘ , and show that any solution Jglobal of (b) leads to:
   (b′ ) a der(t̂h,n )-valued section JΘ of Θ∗ over Th,n such that
   (c′ ) dΘ − JΘ is flat in a sense to be explained therein, and
   (d′ ) the restriction of JΘ to ker(T ϖ) coincides with the image of JDHS outlined in (d).

                                              7
   We will construct the connection dΘ − JΘ with the properties (b′ ), (c′ ), (d′ ) in sub-
sequent papers in the series and refer to it as the solution to the global (1, 0) extension
problem as the extension is only in the (1, 0) direction on Th .
    In this paper, we shall choose to replace the setting of the fibration Th,n → Th by a
trivialized setting, working locally on the product Cf n (Σ) × Th instead of Th,n . In this
setting, we may assemble the DHS connections at given complex structures into a family
of connections on this product structure as follows. We shall denote by CS(Σ) the space
of complex structures on Σ and by Diff 0 (Σ) the group of diffeomorphisms connected to
the identity. An arbitrary local holomorphic section,

                                          s : Th → CS(Σ)                                    (1.2)
                                                                                        s
of the projection CS(Σ) → CS(Σ)/Diff 0 (Σ) = Th gives rise to a vertical 1-form JDHS        on

Cf n (Σ)×Th ; namely, the restriction of JDHS to Cf n (Σ)×{τ } is the DHS one-form attached
to the complex structure s(τ ). Another such section s′ is related to s by a Th -dependent
diffeomorphism, namely, by a local map ϕ : Th → Diff 0 (Σ) such that s′ (τ ) = s(τ )ϕ(τ )
for τ ∈ Th (henceforth simply denoted s′ = s · ϕ). The domain Uss′ of the map ϕ is the
intersection of the domains of s and s′ . The vertical 1-forms JDHSs

and ∂¯Th according to the decomposition (1.5). It turns out that for any ϕ : Th → Diff 0 (Σ),
the diffeomorphism ϕn of Cf n (Σ) × Th lifts to this bundle.4

1.3.1       Formulation of the local (1, 0) extension problem
Taking advantage of the product structure Cf n (Σ) × Th , the problem then becomes the
local (1, 0) extension problem, which may be formulated as follows;
 (b′′ ) for any section s, to construct5 a der(t̂h,n )-valued local section Ls of T ∗(1,0) Th over
        Cf n (Σ)×Th , in such a way that for any sections s and s′ related by a diffeomorphism
                              ′
        s′ = s · ϕ, one has Ls = ϕ∗n Ls ;
 (c′′ ) the connection component J s defined by,
                                                J s = JDHS
                                                       s
                                                           + Ls                                      (1.8)
         corresponds to a flat connection dCf n (Σ) + ∂Th − J s .
   We shall pick local coordinates (x|τ ) on Cf n (Σ) × Th , where x = (x1 , · · · , xn ) are local
coordinates on Cf n (Σ) and τ = (τ1 , · · · , τσ ) with σ = dim Th are local coordinates on Th ,
with dim Th = 3h − 3 for h ≥ 2 and dim T1 = 1. The flatness condition (c′′ ) may then be
expressed more explicitly on J s = J s (x|τ ) by,6
                                (dx + ∂τ ) J s − J s ∧ J s = 0                          (1.9)
where dx = i=1 (dxi ∂xi + dx̄i ∂x̄i ) and ∂τ = σα=1 dτα ∂τα represent the total differential

dCf n (Σ) and the Dolbeault differential ∂Th , respectively. Taking into account the flatness
of the DHS connection itself, the flatness condition (c′′ ) becomes equivalent to a set of
partial differential equations for Ls , given by,

1.4     The main result
The main result of this paper is stated in the following theorem.7

Theorem 1. For any τ 0 ∈ Th , there exists a neighborhood Uτ 0 of τ 0 in which the local
(1, 0) extension problem, formulated in items (b′′ ) and (c′′ ) of subsection 1.3.1, admits a
solution, namely, for an arbitrary section s : Uτ 0 → CS(Σ), there exists Ls as in (b′′ ) and
satisfying (c′′ ). In local coordinates, this means that the differential equation (1.10) has a
solution taking values in der(t̂h,n ) that satisfies condition (b′′ ) upon changing sections.

   The proof of Theorem 1 will be presented in section 7 by combining the results of
Theorems 7.3 and 7.4. In fact, the components Lα = Lsα of the solution (see (1.11)) are
obtained in explicit form given in Corollary 4.20, given there on the generators of t̂h,n and
extended to the entire algebra by the results of section 6.


1.5     Further results, methods and organization
Henceforth, we shall drop the superscript s referring to a choice of section unless indicated
otherwise. To solve (1.10) for L requires evaluating the moduli variation ∂τ JDHS . In
section 3, we review the theory of complex structure variations in terms of Beltrami and
quadratic differentials. Section 3 then provides explicit formulas for the variations of
Abelian differentials ωI , the Arakelov Green function G(x, y), the DHS kernels8 and the
connection JDHS . The results may all be expressed in terms of DHS kernels.
    In section 4, we obtain necessary conditions to be satisfied by a potential solution to
(1.9). These necessary conditions may be used to produce explicit expressions for the
action of L on the generators of t̂h,n , assuming that L exists. The explicit form of L is
given in (4.15), the constituents Lα of L decompose into Lα = L0α + L1α with L1α given
in (4.56) and the action of L0α on the generators of th,n is given by (4.55). This reveals

   7
    One can show that a solution JΘ of (b′ ) gives rise to a solution s 7→ J s by J s = triv∗s JΘ . In
subsequent work, we intend to construct such a solution of (b′ ) and to relate it in this way with the
solution presently found for (b′′ ).
   8
    The DHS kernels are the coefficients of the expansion of JDHS as a Lie series in the Lie algebra t̂h,n .
They are single-valued real analytic functions and differential 1-forms out of which the polylogarithms
associated with the connection JDHS can be constructed with the help of iterated integrals [2], whence
the name DHS integration kernels, or simply DHS kernels. They will be defined in section 2.1, and their
moduli variations will be given in section 3.5.3.

the functional dependence of L in terms of DHS kernels as well as differentials ∂τ A and
∂τ D of new modular tensors A and D. This functional dependence of L in terms of ∂τ A
and ∂τ D is the counterpart to the functional dependence of JDHS in terms of the DHS
kernels. The modular tensors A and D are built from the convolution integrals of DHS
kernels, and their components, which are single-valued real-analytic functions on Th , are
given explicitly as follows,9

Sp(2h, Z)) are given in Theorem 4.16. The modular tensors A and D obey a wealth of
linear relations, some of which are given in Proposition 2.2 of section 2.
    In sections 5, 6 and 7, we shall show that the necessary local conditions on L obtained
in section 4 are actually sufficient for the existence and flatness of a local connection J .
A major task will be to demonstrate in section 6 that L takes values in der(t̂h,n ). For this
purpose, th,n will be uplifted to a larger algebra uh,n in intermediate steps. The corre-
sponding extended components of the local connection J are shown in section 5 to take
values in the Lie algebra der(ûh,n ) before obtaining the desired J ∈ der(t̂h,n ), through the
Lie algebra projection uh,n → th,n , and proving in section 7 that J , or more precisely the
assignment s 7→ J s where s is a local section as in section 1.2, satisfies the flatness condi-
tions (1.10), as summarized in Theorem 7.3. Together with the extendability established
in Theorem 7.4, we arrive at the main result of this work previewed in Theorem 1.
    In Proposition 7.5, we identify modular properties of Ls . This partially10 sets the
stage for discussing modularity properties of the assignment s 7→ J s .
     9
    The rank 4 modular tensor AM KL N was introduced by Kawazumi in [14, 15]. Its double trace
A KL K is proportional to the Kawazumi-Zhang invariant [25, 26] which, in the special case of genus two,
 L

plays an important role in the low energy expansion of Type IIB string theory [27, 28, 29].
  10
     The other ingredients of this discussion, namely the construction of group-valued cocycles on the
space Cf n (Σ) × CS(Σ) and discussion of their behavior under the diffeomorphisms (ϕss′ )n , will be treated
in forthcoming work.

                                                    11
    It was established in [30] that the non-trivial components of the flatness conditions on
the multi-variable DHS connection JDHS are equivalent to the union of all interchange and
Fay identities, namely non-trivial bilinear relations between the DHS kernels out of which
JDHS is constructed, that were derived via other methods in [31]. The flat connection J
extends JDHS by including the directions in which moduli are varied, with corresponding
integration kernels ∂τ A and ∂τ D. The flatness conditions on J impose linear and bilinear
relations between ∂τ A, ∂τ D and the familiar DHS kernels. A preliminary account of these
relations is given in section 8, but a systematic analysis is relegated to future work.
    In section 9, we give a detailed account of the global flat connection J in the spe-
cial case of genus one. We discuss how the Lie algebra of the Calaque-Enriquez-Etingof
connection [12] emerges and show its relation to Tsunogai’s derivation algebra [32], ex-
plaining why the contributions to J in Remark 4.18 are viewed as generalizing Tsunogai’s
derivations to arbitrary genus.
   Nine appendices elaborate on technical aspects of this work, with combinatorial pre-
requisites in appendix A, coincident limits of Fay identities in appendix B and several
proofs in the remaining appendices.


1.6       Ongoing work
In this series of papers dedicated to the topic of Flat connections on moduli spaces, of
which this paper is the first, we plan to extend the scope of the present work by the
investigations of the following problems,

      • the global (1, 0) extension problem for the DHS connection;
      • the full global extension problem, including the (0, 1), namely, the dτ̄α analogue of L
        arising from the trivialization of Jglobal ;
      • the extension problem in the meromorphic context of the11 Enriquez connection [4].


1.7       Future directions
An important long-term goal is the construction of homotopy-invariant iterated integrals
based on the flat connections J and Jglobal . The flatness of Jglobal on the space Th,n
guarantees that the path-ordered exponential of Jglobal along a path γ ⊂ Th,n ,
                                             Z
                                Γ[γ] = Pexp Jglobal                               (1.14)
                                                    γ


 11
      See also [33], p. 669, footnote 1.

                                               12
is homotopy invariant and may be utilized to investigate the moduli dependence of poly-
logarithms on Cf n (Σ).
    More specifically, by equating the path-ordered exponential of (1.14) associated with
different paths γ1 and γ2 with the same endpoints (see figure 2), polylogarithms may be
expressed via iterated integrals over modular tensors in complex structure moduli. These
representations provide powerful tools to unravel the relations among special values of
polylogarithms at arbitrary genus in the same way as the systematics of elliptic poly-
logarithms at rational points on a torus is governed by iterated integrals over modular
forms [34, 35]. In the special case of paths γ1 or γ2 in the fundamental group of Cf n (Σ),
flatness of Jglobal implies iterated-integral representations for higher-genus versions of mul-
tiple zeta values [36] and associators [37, 2, 38, 39], generalizing the genus one results on
elliptic multiple zeta values [40, 41, 42] and elliptic associators [12, 43].

                                                            Th,n
                                          γ1




                                                γ2


Figure 2: Flatness of Jglobal guarantees equality of the path-ordered exponentials Γ[γ1 ] =
Γ[γ2 ] for any pair of homotopic curves γ1 and γ2 in Th,n .


    Moreover, combining the path-ordered exponentials of Jglobal in (1.14) with their com-
plex conjugates will offer a starting point to investigate iterated-integral representations
of the non-holomorphic modular tensors in [14, 15, 18, 19] and the systematics of their
relations. In this way, Jglobal is expected to be a stepping stone to explore echoes beyond
genus one of the link [44, 45, 46] between modular graph forms [16, 17] and equivariant
iterated Eisenstein integrals [47, 48].

                                           14
2      The DHS connection on a fixed Riemann surface
In this section, we review the DHS connection in n ≥ 2 variables on a fixed compact
Riemann surface Σ of genus h ≥ 1 without punctures, including the DHS integration
kernels and the structure relations of the infinite-dimensional Lie algebra t̂h,n from which
the connection is constructed. Since the DHS connection in n variables on a surface
with p punctures may be obtained from the connection in n + p variables on a surface
without punctures by freezing p of the variables, there is no need to consider the case
with punctures separately [3].



2.1     The DHS kernels on a Riemann surface Σ
Throughout this section, we shall consider a fixed compact Riemann surface Σ of arbitrary
genus h ≥ 1 without punctures. The homology group H1 (Σ, Z) is equipped with a non-
degenerate intersection pairing J with respect to which a canonical basis of cycles AI
and BI has intersection pairings J(AI , AJ ) = J(BI , BJ ) = 0 and J(AI , BJ ) = δJI for
I, J ∈ {1, · · · , h}. Choosing loops AI and BI on Σ with a common base point p ∈ Σ
to represent these cycles may be used to promote them to generators of the fundamental
group π1 (Σ, p). A basis of the Dolbeault cohomology group H (1,0) (Σ, C) is given by h
holomorphic (1, 0)-forms ωI normalized by their A-periods and whose B-periods give the
components ΩIJ of the period matrix Ω,

                             I                              I
                                   ωJ = δJI                        ωJ = ΩIJ                           (2.1)
                              AI                              BI



Similarly, a basis of H (0,1) (Σ, C) is given by the anti-holomorphic (0, 1) forms ω̄I . The
Riemann relations ensure symmetry of Ω and positivity of Im (Ω). The pull-back of the
translation invariant Kähler form on the Jacobian Ch /(Zh +ΩZh ) to Σ provides a canonical
volume form κ on Σ, written in local complex coordinates12 as κ(x) d2 x, which may be


  12
       Throughout, we shall equivalently represent a globally defined differential form in terms of local
coordinates with the help of local coefficient functions that have suitable transformation properties under
a change of coordinates. For example, Abelian differentials ωI are expressed locally as ωI = ωI (x)dx in
terms of coefficient function ωI (x). The total differential d on Σ decomposes into d = ∂ + ∂¯ where the
Dolbeault differentials are expressed as ∂ = dx ∂x and ∂¯ = dx̄ ∂x̄ in local complex coordinates x, x̄ where
∂x , ∂x̄ stand for partial derivatives (see section 3.1 below for more details). TheR coordinate volume form
will be denoted d2 x = 2i dx ∧ dx̄ and the Dirac delta function is normalized by Σ d2 x δ(x, y) = 1.

normalized as follows,13

                            1
                      κ(x) = ωI (x)ω̄ I (x)                                   d2 x κ(x) = 1               (2.2)
                            h                                             Σ


The Arakelov Green function G(x, y) is a symmetric single-valued function of x, y ∈ Σ,
which is smooth outside the diagonal, has a logarithmic singularity at x = y, and is
uniquely determined by the following equations (for an explicit construction see [18, 2]),
                                                                              Z
          ∂x̄ ∂x G(x, y) = −πδ(x, y) + πκ(x)                                       d2 x κ(x)G(x, y) = 0   (2.3)

In terms of the Arakelov Green function G∅ (x, y) = G(x, y) we define the following smooth

                                              Z

The function Φ is traceless in its last two indices, while G satisfies a reflection symmetry,

               ΦI1 ···Ir−1 J J (x) = 0                      GI1 ···Ir (x, y) = (−)r GIr ···I1 (y, x)      (2.5)

The DHS kernels for r ≥ 1 are given in terms of G and Φ by,14

                       fI1 ···Ir J (x, y) = ∂x ΦI1 ···Ir J (x) − ∂x GI1 ···Ir−1 (x, y) δIJr               (2.6)

and inherit their single-valuedness in x, y ∈ Σ from the Arakelov Green functions in the
convolutions (2.4). Conversely, the functions ∂x Φ and ∂x G may be obtained from the
traceless and trace parts of f , respectively. It will often be convenient to amend these
relations by setting f∅ J (x, y) = ∂x Φ∅ J (x) = ω J (x) for the case corresponding to r = 0.

  13
    Throughout, a pair of a repeated upper and lower index is to be summed over by the Einstein

the metric Y = Im (Ω) and its inverse Y −1 with components YIJ and Y IJ , respectively. For example,
using these notations we have YIJ Y JK = δIK and ω̄ I = Y IJ ω̄J .

                                                          16
2.1.1   Differential relations amongst DHS kernels
The integral relations of (2.4), along with the definition of G(x, y) in (2.3), readily imply
the following Massey system of differential equations for r ≥ 1,

                 ∂x̄ ∂x ΦI1 ···Ir J (x) = −π ω̄I1 (x) ∂x ΦI2 ···Ir J (x) + π δr,1 δIJ1 κ(x)
                ∂x̄ ∂x GI1 ···Ir (x, y) = −π ω̄I1 (x) ∂x GI2 ···Ir (x, y)
                ∂ȳ ∂x GI1 ···Ir (x, y) = −π ω̄K (y) fI1 ···Ir K (x, y)                            (2.7)

The last two equations are extended to r = 0 via (2.3) and,

                            ∂x ∂ȳ G(x, y) = πδ(x, y) − π ω̄K (y)ω K (x)                           (2.8)

For later use, it will be convenient to have the differential equations directly in terms of
the DHS kernels f , which are again given by a Massey type system,

               ∂x̄ fI1 ···Ir J (x, y) = −π ω̄I1 (x) fI2 ···Ir J (x, y) + π δr,1 δIJ1 δ(x, y)
               ∂ȳ fI1 ···Ir J (x, y) = π fI1 ···Ir−1 K (x, y) ω̄K (y)δIJr − π δr,1 δIJ1 δ(x, y)   (2.9)

Further properties of the DHS kernels may be found in [2, 31].
Remark 2.1. The multiplets Φ, G and f defined above all transform under non-linear
tensor representations of the modular group Sp(2h, Z) [19] to be specified in section 2.5
below. The choice to invert the positions of the indices on these tensors, indicated in
footnote 14, is made so that each tensor transforms under Sp(2h, Z) with automorphy
factors (2.31) (which are themselves matrices) that are purely anti-holomorphic in the
moduli of Σ. The advantage of this choice is that holomorphic derivatives in the moduli
are covariant without the need for a connection, as will be detailed in section 3.

2.3     The Lie algebra th,n
The DHS connection in n variables on a compact Riemann surface Σ of genus h ≥ 1
without punctures takes values in the infinite-dimensional Lie algebra t̂h,n .

Definition 2.3. The generators of the Lie algebra th,n are aiI , bIi and tij = tji , for I ∈
{1, · · · , h} and i, j ∈ {1, · · · , n} with j ̸= i and obey the defining structure relations [4],
                                          I J                 I
                                                                 bi , ajJ = δJI tij
                                                                        
              aiI , ajJ = 0               bi , bj = 0
                                          I                                    X
                                                                 biI , aIi = −
                                                                      
                aiI , tjk = 0             bi , tjk = 0                              tij      (2.15)
                                                                                  j̸=i

where i, j, k are mutually distinct. The Lie algebra th,n admits a positive bi-grading, de-
noted | · |, that assigns the bi-degrees |aiI | = (1, 0), |bIi | = (0, 1) and |tij | = (1, 1) to the
generators of th,n . Elements X ∈ th,n of bi-degree |X| = (k, ℓ) are said to have a-degree k
and b-degree ℓ. The Lie algebra t̂h,n is the degree completion of th,n .17
   Further structure relations are obtained from (2.15) using the Jacobi identity [4],
                                                                           
                     aiI + ajI , tij = 0                      tij + tik , tjk = 0
                       I
                       bi + bIj , tij = 0
                                                                            
                                                                     tij , tkℓ = 0            (2.16)

for i, j, k, ℓ mutually distinct. We note that, for genus h ≥ 2, the relations [aiI , tjk ] =
[bIi , tjk ] = 0 of (2.15) may be deduced from the other defining relations.

2.4     The multivariable DHS connection
The multivariable DHS connection JDHS defined in [3] takes values in the Lie algebra t̂h,n
given in Definition 2.3 and is a single-valued one-form of n variables x = (x1 , · · · , xn ) ∈ Σn
which is smooth on the configuration space,

                                     Cfn (Σ) = Σn \ {diagonals}                                     (2.17)

with singularities along the diagonals xi = xj given by holomorphic simple poles with
residue −tij . It will be convenient to locally decompose JDHS into a sum of (1, 0)-forms
and (0, 1)-forms,

      The summation over words I⃗ ∈ Wh represents the double sum over the length r ≥ 0 of the words
I⃗ = I1 · · · Ir and, for given r, the sum over all its individual letters I1 , · · · , Ir ∈ {1, · · · , h}, as illustrated
                                                                                              ⃗
in the right equalities of (2.24) and (2.25). The symbols ΦI⃗J (x), GI⃗(x, y) and B I stand for ΦI1 ···Ir J (x),
                                                        ⃗
GI1 ···Ir (x, y) and for the concatenation product B I = B I1 · · · B Ir , respectively.


                                                            21
2.5    Modular invariance of JDHS
Modular transformations leave the intersection pairing J invariant and map canonical
bases of homology cycles to canonical homology bases. Assembling the cycles AI and BI
into column matrices A, B a modular transformation acts by a matrix M ∈ Sp(2h, Z),
                                                               
            B       B                                         A B
               → M                                M=                                M t JM = J      (2.28)
            A       A                                         C D

Under Sp(2h, Z), the row matrix of holomorphic Abelian differentials ω, the period matrix
Ω of (2.1), and its imaginary part Y = Im (Ω) transform with the following Ω-dependent
automorphy factors that take values in GL(h, C),

                                   ω → ω(CΩ+D)−1
                                   Ω → (AΩ+B)(CΩ+D)−1
                                   Y → (Ω̄C t +Dt )−1 Y (CΩ+D)−1                                    (2.29)

Modular tensors transform under more general non-linear representations of Sp(2h, Z).
Using contractions with the metric Y or with its inverse, we may convert an arbitrary
modular tensor to one whose modular transformation law involves only holomorphic au-
tomorphy factors (which is the choice made in [3]), or to a tensor whose transformation
law involves only anti-holomorphic automorphy factors. Here, we shall make the latter
choice (see Remark 2.1) so that the derivations with respect to holomorphic moduli that
we shall use in the sequel are then covariant without the need for a connection.
     Therefore, more generally, we define a modular tensor T of rank (r, s) with components
TI1 ···Ir J1 ···Js to transform as follows under M ∈ Sp(2h, Z),

          TI1 ···Ir J1 ···Js (Ω) → Q̃I1K1 · · · Q̃IrKr TK1 ···Kr L1 ···Ls (Ω) R̃L1J1 · · · R̃LsJs   (2.30)

where we use shorthands for the automorphy factor Q̃ and its inverse R̃,
                                                                      −1
                                  Q̃ = Q̃(M, Ω) = Ω̄C t +Dt
                                  R̃ = R̃(M, Ω) = Ω̄C t +Dt = Q̃−1                                  (2.31)

As defined in (2.30), modular tensors are sections of an anti-holomorphic vector bundle
over Torelli space (which is the moduli space of compact Riemann surfaces equipped with
a canonical basis of A and B cycles). These conventions are related to the definition of
modular tensors in [19] by complex conjugation and ensure that the holomorphic moduli
derivatives in section 3 preserve tensorial properties.

                                                       22
Lemma 2.5. The DHS kernels Φ and f defined in (2.4) and (2.6) transform as modular
tensors of rank (r, 1), while G transforms as a tensor of rank (r, 0),

                      fI1 ···Ir J (x, y) → Q̃I1 K1 · · · Q̃Ir Kr fK1 ···Kr L (x, y)R̃L J
                       GI1 ···Ir (x, y) → Q̃I1 K1 · · · Q̃Ir Kr GK1 ···Kr (x, y)                       (2.32)

Similarly, the quantities AM P1 ···Pr N , DI1 ···Is and NI1 ···It defined by (2.10) and (2.11) for
r, s, t ≥ 2 transform as modular tensors of rank (r, 2), (s, 0) and (t, 0), respectively.

Proof. The modular tensor properties (2.32) follow from those of ω M , ω̄K together with
modular invariance of G(x, y) and readily imply the tensorial Sp(2h, Z) transformation of
AM P1 ···Pr N , DI1 ···Is and NI1 ···It through their integral representations (2.10) and (2.11).
   The tensorial transformation (2.32) of DHS kernels leads to the following proposition
proven in section 4.5 of [3].

Proposition 2.6. The modular transformation properties of the DHS kernels, given in
(2.32), lead to a modular invariant multi-variable DHS connection JDHS , provided the
generators a, b, t of the Lie algebra th,n transform as follows under M ∈ Sp(2h, Z),

         aiI → Q̃(M, Ω)I K aiK                    bJi → bLi R̃(M, Ω)L J                    tij → tij   (2.33)

These transformations leave the structure relations of th,n given in Definition 2.3 invari-
ant, so that Sp(2h, Z) is an automorphism group of th,n .




                                                     23
3     Moduli variations of the DHS connection
In this section, we shall begin by briefly reviewing the theory of complex structure defor-
mations on Riemann surfaces in sections 3.1 to 3.4. We then proceed by providing explicit
formulas for the variational derivatives of Abelian differentials, the Arakelov Green func-
tion, and the DHS kernels in section 3.5, and for the modular tensors A and D in section
3.6. Combining these results with the vanishing of the variational derivatives of the gen-
erators of the Lie algebra th,n discussed in section 3.7, we obtain the variational derivative
of the multivariable DHS connection in 3.8.


3.1    Complex structures and Teichmüller spaces
Throughout, we shall consider a smooth orientable compact surface Σ of genus h ≥ 1.
An almost complex structure J on Σ maps the tangent space Tp (Σ) to Σ at each point
p ∈ Σ to itself J : Tp (Σ) → Tp (Σ), correspondingly maps the tangent bundle T Σ to itself
J : T Σ → T Σ, and squares to minus the identity map so that J is an automorphism
of both Tp (Σ) and T Σ. Its integrability being automatic, J defines a complex structure
on Σ, so that Σ equipped with J is a Riemann surface. A complex structure J splits
the (complexified) tangent bundle into holomorphic and anti-holomorphic components
          (1,0)          (0,1)
denoted TJ Σ and TJ Σ on which the eigenvalue of J is +i and −i, respectively,
                                                (1,0)        (0,1)
                                  TC Σ = TJ             Σ ⊕ TJ       Σ                   (3.1)

and similarly for the cotangent bundle; we shall often use the notation,
                                ∗(1,0)                                   ∗(0,1)
                        KJ = TJ          Σ                   K J = TJ             Σ      (3.2)

for their dual bundles. This splitting depends upon the complex structure, as indicated
by the subscript J. The total differential d splits accordingly,

                                             d = ∂J + ∂¯J                                (3.3)

and this splitting again depends on J, as indicated by the subscript J.
    A complex structure on Σ is equivalent to a conformal structure on Σ which, in turn,
is specified by a Riemannian metric g modulo Weyl transformations g → eϕ g. In real
local coordinates ξ m with m ∈ {1, 2}, the metric may be expressed as g = gmn dξ m dξ n
while the complex structure J is given by [49, 50],
                                            p
                                 Jm n = εmp det g g pn                             (3.4)

                                                   24
where g pn are the components of the inverse of g so that gmn g np = δm  p
                                                                           while εmn = −εnm
with ε12 = 1 specifies the orientation of Σ. In these coordinates, the components ∂J and
∂ J of the splitting of the total differential d = ∂J + ∂ J introduced in (3.3) are given by,

                  1                                                      1                  
              ∂J = dξ m ∂m − iJm n ∂n                                 ∂ J = dξ m ∂m + iJm n ∂n                   (3.5)
                  2                                                        2

with ∂m = ∂/∂ξ m . The local coordinates ξ m are arbitrary and may be chosen to be
independent of the complex structure. Instead, however, we may choose local coordi-
nates adapted to the complex structure J in which both J and the metric g take simple
forms. Such a choice of coordinates will now depend on J. We choose real adapted local
coordinates ξ m in which the components of the complex structure J are given by,

                              J1 1 = J2 2 = 0                         J1 2 = −J2 1 = 1                           (3.6)

and the metric takes the form g = eϕ |dξ 1 + idξ 2 |2 . Correspondingly, in terms of the
complex adapted local coordinates z = ξ 1 + iξ 2 and z̄ = ξ 1 − iξ 2 the components of the
complex structure are given by,19

                               Jz z = −Jz̄ z̄ = i                      Jz z̄ = Jz̄ z = 0                         (3.7)

and the metric takes the form g = eϕ |dz|2 . Equation (3.7) reflects the fact that diago-
nalizing J of (3.6) acting on T Σ requires complexification to TC Σ, thereby extending the
validity of (3.5) to arbitrary real or complex local coordinates. The differential operators
∂J and ∂ J reduce to the familiar expressions ∂J = dz ∂z and ∂¯J = dz̄ ∂z̄ so that locally
holomorphic functions f satisfy the familiar Cauchy-Riemann equation ∂¯J f = 0.
    We denote by CS(Σ) the infinite-dimensional complex manifold of complex structures
on Σ. The infinite-dimensional Lie group Diff 0 (Σ) of diffeomorphisms of Σ that are
continuously connected to the identity naturally acts on CS(Σ). The Teichmüller space
of genus h is obtained from CS(Σ) by the quotient,

                                             Th = CS(Σ)/Diff 0 (Σ)                                               (3.8)

The Teichmüller space Th is a contractible finite-dimensional Kähler manifold, whose
dimensions are dimC T1 = 1 and dimC Th = 3h − 3 for h ≥ 2 (see, for example, [10] and
references therein). The manifold structure of Th may be obtained out of (3.8) since the
action of Diff 0 (Σ) is free, continuous, proper, and with local sections [51].

                                                                  1    ′   2
 19
      The relation between J of (3.6) and (3.7) is Jξ1 +ηiξ2 ξ +η iξ = 12 (J1 1 + iηJ1 2 − iη ′ J2 1 + ηη ′ J2 2 ).


                                                          25
3.2    Variations of complex structure
An infinitesimal variation of the complex structure J may be parametrized by,

                                   Jm n → Jm n + ϵ δJm n                                 (3.9)

subject to the condition that the deformed complex structure squares to minus the identity
to leading order in the infinitesimal parameter ϵ,

                                 Jm p δJp n + δJm p Jp n = 0                            (3.10)

In terms of the complex adapted local coordinates in which J takes the form of (3.7),
this condition requires δJz z = δJz̄ z̄ = 0. Recall from (3.1) that J splits the complexified
                                               (1,0)       (0,1)
tangent bundle TC Σ into two sub-bundles TJ Σ and TJ Σ on which J takes the eigen-
                                                                                       (1,0)
values +i and −i, respectively. Complex conjugation exchanges the sub-bundles TJ Σ
       (0,1)
and TJ Σ of TC Σ, as well as their duals.
    The infinitesimal variation δJ is a linear map from the complexified tangent bundle
TC Σ to itself which can be equivalently described as the pair (δJz̄ z , δJz z̄ ) of mutually
complex conjugate maps between the two sub-bundles,
                                               (
                                                          (1,0)  (0,1)
                                                δJz̄ z : TJ Σ → TJ Σ
               δJ : TC Σ → TC Σ                           (0,1)  (1,0)                 (3.11)
                                                δJz z̄ : TJ Σ → TJ Σ

                                                                              (1,0)     ∗(0,1)
The maps δJz̄ z and δJz z̄ may also be viewed as sections of the bundles TJ Σ ⊗ TJ           Σ
      (0,1)     ∗(1,0)
and TJ Σ ⊗ TJ          Σ, respectively, and are referred to as Beltrami differentials µ and µ̄.
In adapted local complex coordinates they are defined by,

                    µ = µ(z) dz̄/dz                      δJz̄ z = −2i µ(z)
                    µ̄ = µ(z) dz/dz̄                     δJz z̄ = +2i µ(z)              (3.12)

The normalization factors are conventional and have been chosen so that, under a variation
δJ of the complex structure J, the metric g = eϕ |dz|2 associated with J maps to the metric
        ′
g ′ = eϕ |dz − ϵµ(z)dz̄|2 associated with J + ϵδJ. Thus, the complex structure variation
δJ is equivalent to a pair (µ, µ̄) of a Beltrami differential and its complex conjugate and
the (real) tangent space TJ (CS(Σ)) to CS(Σ) is generated by all such pairs (µ, µ̄).
   The (complexified) tangent space to CS(Σ) at J splits into its holomorphic and anti-
holomorphic components,
                                     (1,0)          (0,1)       
                  TJ CS(Σ) ⊗ C = TJ         CS(Σ) ⊕ TJ      CS(Σ)                (3.13)

                                              26
                       (1,0)                  (0,1)
Its component TJ (CS(Σ)) and TJ (CS(Σ)) are generated by the pairs of Beltrami
differentials (µ, 0) and (0, µ̄), respectively, namely,20
                             (1,0)                 (1,0) ∗(0,1) 
                          TJ       CS(Σ) = Γ Σ, TJ Σ ⊗ TJ       Σ
                             (0,1)                 (0,1) ∗(1,0) 
                          TJ       CS(Σ) = Γ Σ, TJ Σ ⊗ TJ       Σ       (3.14)
relative to the (1, 0) ⊕ (0, 1) decomposition of (3.1). We also use the notation
                                             (1,0)      ∗(0,1) 
                               BDJ := Γ Σ, TJ Σ ⊗ TJ          Σ                                      (3.15)

3.4     Diffeomorphisms and derivatives with respect to moduli
We shall mostly be interested in functions on the space of complex structures CS(Σ)
that are invariant under the group of diffeomorphisms Diff 0 (Σ). This invariance reduces
their dependence on the complex structure to their dependence on Teichmüller space, as
discussed in section 3.1 above (see also section I.E in [50]).

  22
     The identity may be proven by observing that the relation is ultra-local, namely proportional to
δ(v, w), upon which the remaining components g ww and g vv at w = v are equal to one another so that
their derivatives commute with one another. A more detailed and more rigorous proof will be given in a
subsequent paper in this series.

                                                 28
    The Lie algebra of Diff 0 (Σ) is the space vec(Σ) of all smooth vector fields on Σ. For each
                                                                                      (1,0)
J ∈ CS(Σ), the action of Diff 0 (Σ) on CS(Σ) induces a linear map vec(Σ) → TJ CS(Σ)
into the space of Beltrami differentials, given by the following J-dependent composition,
                                       (1,0)  ∂           (1,0)     ∗(0,1) 
                  vec(Σ) → Γ Σ, TJ Σ −−J→ Γ Σ, TJ Σ ⊗ TJ                   Σ                (3.25)
which we will also denote ∂¯J . The first
                                        map in (3.25) is the inclusion of vec(Σ) = Γ(Σ, T Σ)
into the space of sections Γ Σ, TC Σ of the complexified tangent bundle TC Σ, which is
then decomposed according to (3.1) and projected onto its holomorphic component,
                                            (1,0)      (0,1)            (1,0)
           Γ Σ, T Σ ⊂ Γ Σ, TC Σ = Γ Σ, TJ Σ ⊕ TJ Σ → Γ Σ, TJ Σ)                        (3.26)
The second map in (3.25) is given by the ∂¯J operator associated with the holomorphic
                       (1,0)
bundle structure of TJ Σ.
    Translated into concrete formulas, we represent an element of vec(Σ) by a vector
field v = v m ∂m in real coordinates ξ m , complexify v to v(w)∂w + v̄(w)∂w̄ , project to the
holomorphic component v(w)∂w , and map this into the space of Beltrami differentials. The
action of vec(Σ) on the complex structure J is given by the following Beltrami differential,
                                       µ(w) = ∂w̄ v(w)                                     (3.27)
The formula may also be derived from the well-known action of a vector field v m on the
metric, modulo Weyl transformations. Note that, for genus h ≥ 2, there are no solutions
to ∂w̄ v(w) = 0, namely, Σ has no conformal Killing vectors and no continuous symmetries.
    Viewing a function ψ on Teichmüller space Th as a Diff 0 (Σ) invariant function on the
space of complex structures CS(Σ), its invariance under vec(Σ) combined with the pairing
relation (3.18) implies that,
                                         ¯ ww ψ⟩ = 0
                                        ⟨∂v|δ                                              (3.28)
for all v ∈ vec(Σ). Since the pairing is non-degenerate, this implies that δww ψ is a
holomorphic quadratic differential. The (1,0) differential of ψ on Th corresponds to a
Diff 0 (Σ)-equivariant assignment that maps J ∈ CS(Σ) to a holomorphic quadratic dif-
ferential δww ψ given by the relation (3.18) for an arbitrary Beltrami differential µ. In
particular, if (τ1 , · · · , τσ ) with σ = dimC (Th ) is a local system of complex coordinates on
Th in a neighborhood of (the equivalence class [J]) of J, then the derivative of ψ with
respect to a holomorphic modulus τα , for α ∈ {1, · · · , σ}, is given by,
                                                        Z
                               ∂                     1
                                   ψ = ⟨µα |δww ψ⟩ =       d2 w µα (w) δww ψ               (3.29)
                              ∂τα                    π Σ
              (1,0)
where µα ∈ TJ (CS(Σ)) is an arbitrary Beltrami differential representing the vector field
∂/∂τα on the Teichmüller space Th .

                                               29
Remark 3.1. The problem of computing moduli variations of an arbitrary function ϕ
has been reduced to evaluating the variational derivative δww ϕ which is a (2, 0) form in a
point w on Σ. We note that, in two-dimensional conformal field theory, the variational
derivative is obtained by inserting the traceless stress tensor Tww into the expectation value
of an arbitrary operator O with the following normalization δww ⟨O⟩ = ⟨Tww O⟩. This can
also be seen from the representation (3.20) of δww as a functional derivative with respect
to the metric. The splitting of the tangent and cotangent bundles of (3.1) was articulated
in [52] and used extensively in [55, 56]. The use of the traceless stress tensor to vary
moduli also dates back to [52] and was elaborated in [49, 53] and reviewed in [50].


3.5     Evaluation of auxiliary variational derivatives
In this section, we shall evaluate the variational derivatives of various key ingredients
in the DHS connection, including local coordinate derivatives, Abelian differentials, the
canonical volume form, the Arakelov Green function, and the DHS integration kernels.
   Converting the moduli variations of Cauchy-Riemann operators of (3.24) into their
variational derivatives gives the following relations,
                                                                                  
              δww ∂z = 0                      δww ∂z̄ = πδ(w, z)∂z + πn ∂z δ(w, z)       (3.30)

We recall that the left formula applies to the derivative ∂z acting on scalars, while the
right formula applies to the ∂z̄ derivative acting on the coefficient function of a (n, 0) form.

3.5.2   The Arakelov Green function
The variational derivative of the prime form was obtained in [53]. The variational deriva-
tive of the Arakelov Green function is given by the following proposition.

Proposition 3.2. The variational derivative of the Arakelov Green function is given by,

                 δww G(x, y) = −∂w G(w, x) ∂w G(w, y) + γww (x) + γww (y)              (3.37)

where γww (x) is given in terms of the DHS kernel GI (x, y) by,

                                        1

   This proposition will be proven in appendix D using the explicit construction of the
Arakelov Green function in terms of Abelian differentials and the prime form in [18].

3.7      Variational derivatives of Lie algebra generators of th,n
Modular invariance of the DHS connection JDHS , which was defined in Theorem 2.4,
requires the automorphy factors Q̃, R̃ of Proposition 2.6 in the modular transformation
law of the generators aiI , bIi and tij of th,n . As a result, the generators aiI and bIi may be
viewed as sections of a non-trivial anti-holomorphic vector bundle over Teichmüller space
on which the variational derivative δww acts covariantly without the need for a connection,

                  δww aiI → Q̃I K (δww aiK )              δww bIi → (δww bK
                                                                          i )R̃K
                                                                                 I
                                                                                             (3.44)

where R̃ = Ω̄C t + Dt and Q̃ = R̃−1 were given in (2.31). Covariance of the variational
derivative allows us to set these derivatives to zero in a manner that is consistent with
modular invariance of JDHS , as summarized by the definition below.

Definition 3.5. We define the variational derivative δww of the generators of the Lie
algebra th,n to vanish,

                δww aiI = 0                 δww bIi = 0                δww tij = 0           (3.45)

This assignment is consistent with the structure relations of th,n .

                                                   34
4        Extending the DHS connection by varying moduli
The main goal of this paper, and the topic of the present and subsequent sections, is to
provide an explicit construction of a local flat connection that extends the multivariable
DHS connection JDHS on the configuration space Cf n (Σ) of n variables on a fixed Riemann
surface Σ by including holomorphic variations in the complex structure of Σ.

4.1        Choice of local sections and strategy
Fix a point τ 0 ∈ Th in Teichmüller space and a complex structure J0 ∈ CS(Σ) above τ 0 .
As explained in section 1.3, a central role is played in Theorem 1 by local sections

                                              s : Th → CS(Σ)                               (4.1)

such that s(τ 0 ) = J0 .
   To a local section s, given in (4.1), and a point τ ∈ Th , is attached the space of
Beltrami differentials BDs,τ defined as the image of the differential dτ s : Tτ Th → BDs(τ )
using the notation (3.15). A basis (eα )α of Tτ Th being given, the space BDs,τ associated
with (s, τ ) is spanned by the Beltrami differentials µsα defined by,

                                        µs,τ
                                         α = (dτ s)(eα ) ∈ BDs(τ )                         (4.2)

where α ∈ {1, · · · , dim Th }.
    In order to be in a position where we can easily translate the problem in terms of
coordinates, we will work not with just one but with several sections. For each tuple
of open subsets U = (U1 , · · · , Un ) of Σ such that the union of their closures does not
cover Σ, namely ∪i U i ̸= Σ,23 we will construct a section sU , with the property that the
corresponding Beltrami differentials satisfy,
                       s ,τ
                      µαU (x) = 0       for all τ and x ∈ Ui with i ∈ {1, · · · , n}       (4.3)

and all α ∈ {1, · · · , dim Th }. The vanishing condition (4.3) for the Beltrami differentials is
advantageous to prevent a mixing of ∂xi and ∂x̄i in the differential equations of DHS kernels
and related quantities under complex structure variations. The existence of sections that
enable (4.3) as well as their construction are discussed in section 4.2 below.
   In sections 4.3 and 4.4 through Lemma 4.11, we shall work locally in the following
                                                                                       sU
sense. We fix a collection of tuples of open sets U = (U1 , · · · , Un ), work with JDHS    , and
                                                                                          sU
study the system of partial differential equations satisfied by the extension of JDHS by

  23
       The relevance of this condition will be made clear in section 4.2.

                                                      35
choosing coordinates (x|τ ) on U1 ×· · ·×Un ×Th , where x = (x1 , · · · , xn ) is a set of complex
coordinates for U1 × ... × Un and τ = (τ1 , · · · , τσ ) is a set of complex coordinates on Th .
Suppressing the superscript sU for clarity, the problem to be solved in those sections will
therefore be the construction of a (1, 0)-form component L(x|τ ) in dτα directions such
that the connection,

                               J (x|τ ) = JDHS (x|τ ) + L(x|τ )                             (4.4)

satisfies the following flatness condition,

                                  (dx + ∂τ )J − J ∧ J = 0                                   (4.5)

expressed in terms of the total differential
                                      P      dx on Cf n (Σ) defined after (2.21) and the differ-
ential in holomorphic moduli ∂τ = α dτα ∂τα (excluding the anti-holomorphic variations
P
  α dτ̄α ∂τ̄α ). The flatness condition (2.21) satisfied by JDHS together with the flatness
condition (4.5) satisfied by J imply the differential equation for the component L,

               (dx + ∂τ )L + dτ JDHS − JDHS ∧ L − L ∧ JDHS − L ∧ L = 0                      (4.6)

This differential equation (in the variables x, x̄, and τ ) satisfies the Frobenius integrability
conditions, as may be shown by applying the differential dx + ∂τ to the left side of (4.6),
eliminating (dx + ∂τ )L using (4.6), and using the flatness condition (2.21).
    While integrability of (4.6) guarantees the existence of local solutions for L, it does
not guarantee that these local solutions extend to regular global single-valued solutions
on the Teichmüller space Th,n of compact Riemann surfaces of genus h with n marked
points. In the remainder of this section, we shall pinpoint necessary conditions for the
existence of a single-valued and modular invariant solution for L and we shall exhibit the
solution to these necessary conditions explicitly in terms of DHS kernels.


4.2    Construction of local sections
In this subsection, we shall provide an explicit construction of the local sections s : Th →
CS(Σ) in (4.1), subject to the conditions of (4.3) on their Beltrami differentials, for a
given tuple U = (U1 , · · · , Un ) of open subsets of Σ with ∪i U i ̸= Σ. This will justify the
possibility to impose the vanishing conditions (4.3) on Beltrami differentials that facilitate
the computations in the remainder of this work.
    The space CS(Σ) of complex structures may be identified with the space P(Σ) of
endomorphisms p of the complexified tangent bundle TC Σ, such that p2 = p = p taking J
to p = (J + i)/(2i). The corresponding bijection between their tangent spaces is induced

                                               36
as follows. Recall that BDJ is the space of Beltrami differentials µ at complex structure
J (see 3.15) and denote by Xp the following space,

                                 Xp = {δq : pδq = δq(1 − p) = 0}                                       (4.7)

at endomorphism p ∈ P(Σ). The tangent spaces to CS(Σ) and P(Σ) are given by,24

                   TJ CS(Σ) = BD∆

iK J are the projection and injection of TC∗ Σ to and from the relevant summands of its
decomposition KJ ⊕ K J .

4.3     The generalized flatness conditions
The flatness conditions of J in (4.4) consist of the flatness conditions of JDHS at fixed
moduli, given in (2.22), supplemented by the conditions in (4.6) that involve the compo-
nents Lα in the directions of holomorphic moduli variations,

where the second equation follows from (4.3), and the arguments (x|τ ) are suppressed

in the preceding subsection.
    It remains to specify the Lie algebra in which the connection J takes values. Clearly
this algebra must include t̂h,n in which JDHS is valued. Two options present themselves.
    In a first option, the connection J takes values in t̂h,n . The components Lα may
be obtained by integrating the system of equations (4.16) with the help of the path-
ordered exponential of the connection JDHS and its associated polylogarithms [2]. This
construction produces Lα in terms of non-local expressions in DHS kernels which preserve
neither the a-grading of t̂h,n nor the single-valuedness of JDHS on Cf n (Σ).

Schemata 4.1. The second option is the one we shall choose and is formulated as fol-
lows. The connection takes values in the Lie algebra der(t̂h,n ) of derivations that contains
t̂h,n as a non-trivial subalgebra and inherits the bi-grading of t̂h,n by a- and b-degrees in
Definition 2.3. The connection is assumed to preserve the a-grading by assigning a-degree
one to Lα and splitting the flatness conditions of (4.16) into their parts of fixed a-degree,

                                                   (1,0)
                                      ∂i Lα − ∂α Ji        =0                          (4.17a)
                                        ∂α Lβ − ∂β Lα = 0                              (4.17b)
                                             (1,0)
                                           [Ji , Lα ] = 0                              (4.17c)
                                              [Lα , Lβ ] = 0                           (4.17d)
                                                   (0,1)
                                              ∂α Ji        =0                          (4.17e)
                                              (0,1)
                                   ∂¯i Lα − [Ji , Lα ] = 0                             (4.17f)

                                                                                           (1,0)
In the sequel, we shall show that the system of equations (4.17), in which der(t̂h,n ), Ji
      (0,1)
and Ji      are considered as given and Lα as the unknowns, may be solved for functions
Lα that are single-valued on Th,n and may be expressed in terms of DHS kernels. In
particular, assuming Lα to have a-degree one will lead to a unique solution of (4.17).

Remark 4.2. The second option generalizes the construction for genus one in terms
of meromorphic and multiple-valued Kronecker-Eisenstein integration kernels [12] and
Tsunogai’s derivation algebra [32]. Here, by contrast, the connection will be single-valued
and modular invariant but non-meromorphic. Its restriction to genus one will be presented
in explicit form in section 9.

compact Riemann surface Σ is solved by Lww − Vww being independent of x. Since Vww
                                                                    0
is independent of x, there exists an x-independent (2, 0)w form Lww such that Lww given
by (4.24) solves (4.19a).

                                              41
Corollary 4.5. The decomposition of Lww into L0ww and a t̂h,n -valued solution L1ww to
(4.19a) is unique modulo shifts (L0ww , L1ww ) → (L0ww + ℓww , L1ww − ℓww ) for an arbitrary
x-independent (2, 0)w -form ℓww that takes values in t̂h,n .
Proof. The shift by an arbitrary x-independent (2, 0)w form ℓww with values in t̂h,n leaves
the decomposition (4.24) invariant. Conversely, the x-dependence of Lww is entirely con-
tained in L1ww so that any shift by ℓww must be x-independent. Since L1ww takes values in
t̂h,n so must ℓww , which establishes uniqueness modulo shifts as specified above.
    The result of the following corollary will play a key role in solving the second differential
flatness condition (4.19b) below.

Remark 4.8. The (2, 0)w form Uww in (4.34) may be expressed as the δww variational
derivative of a function that is neither single-valued on Cf n (Σ) nor well-defined on Mh .
Thus, we expect Uww to correspond to a non-trivial cohomology class in H 1 (Mh , C) with
values in t̂h,n .

Schemata 4.9. At this point in the development of the paper, we have obtained the full x-

in (4.25) and (4.26) and thereby integrated the differential equations (4.19a), (4.21) and
(4.23) for Lww , as well as (4.30) for L1ww . It now remains to determine the x-independent
part L0ww of (4.24).


4.6    Action of L0ww on the generators of th,n
We shall now initiate the determination of the x-independent component L0ww in the
decomposition (4.24) of Lww . Our procedure is as follows.

  1. In this subsection, we derive a number of necessary conditions on L0ww required by
     the system of equations (4.20a) and (4.23);
  2. In the next sections 5 to 7, we shall prove that these necessary conditions are
     sufficient conditions to solve (4.19b) and (4.20), to prove the existence of L0ww and
     to show that it takes values in der(t̂h,n ).

relations (4.53) lead us to view the expansion coefficients of L0ww as generalizations of
Tsunogai’s derivations on t1,n from genus one to arbitrary genus.

4.8      Formulation in terms of moduli variations
Having obtained explicit solutions for L1ww in (4.25) and (4.26) or (4.33) in terms of the
generators of th,n and for the action of L0ww on the generators of th,n in (4.49), we may
now pair these expressions against Beltrami differentials µα (w) to express our results in
terms of the components Lα = L0α + L1α defined by the second equation in (4.18) for each
function and the moduli derivatives of the modular tensors A and D. We note that the
modular tensors A and D have been defined in (2.10) as sections of an anti-holomorphic
vector bundle (see section 2.5) so that the derivatives with respect to holomorphic moduli
τα act covariantly without the need for a connection. The results are as follows.

                                           50
5      Uplifting to a larger algebra
In this section and the next ones, we shall prove that the x-independent part L0ww of the
extended flat connection (4.4) acts as a derivation of t̂h,n and that the remaining flatness
                   (1,0)
conditions [Lww , Ji ] = 0, [Lvv , Lww ] = 0 and δvv Lww − δww Lvv = 0 are satisfied. The
starting point will be the action of L0ww on the generators a, b, t, derived in Theorem 4.16
                                                                (1,0)
from necessary conditions for the flatness condition [Lww , Ji ] = 0 to hold, and the
relations between the modular tensors A and D proven in Proposition 2.2. It turns out,
however, that the flatness conditions require additional relations between A and D which
are akin to the Fay identities between DHS kernels [31], and whose explicit form is as yet
not known explicitly. To circumvent these issues, we shall proceed as follows.


5.1     The Lie algebra uh,n
In this subsection, we introduce an auxiliary Lie algebra uh,n which will provide an essential
stepping stone towards proving, in section 6.3 below, that the action of L0ww in Theorem
4.16 defines a derivation on t̂h,n .

Definition 5.1. The Lie algebra lie(ã, b̃, t̃) is defined to be freely generated by the elements
ãiI , b̃Ii and t̃ij = t̃ji for I ∈ {1, · · · , h} and i, j ∈ {1, · · · , n} with j ̸= i. The Lie algebra
uh,n is the quotient of lie(ã, b̃, t̃) by the following set of relations for mutually distinct
i, j, k,30
                                          I J

The Lie algebra uh,n is equipped with a positive bi-grading |ãiI | = (1, 0), |b̃Ii | = (0, 1)
and |t̃ij | = (1, 1) whose first and second entries are referred to as ã-degree and b̃-degree,
respectively. The degree completion of uh,n is denoted ûh,n . We define the ideal I ⊂ uh,n by,

                          I = ideal generated by [ãiI , ãjJ ] with i ̸= j                        (5.2)

  30
      The relations of (5.1) are the ones used to define th,n in Definition 2.3, except for the relation
[aiI , ajJ ] = 0 of th,n for i ̸= j whose counterpart is being omitted in the definition of uh,n .


                                                    51
The Lie algebra th,n may be obtained as the quotient th,n = uh,n /I via the exact sequence

                                                                 π
                                        0 → I → uh,n −−→ th,n → 0                                                 (5.3)

The projection π : uh,n → th,n maps the generators as follows,

                 π(ãiI ) = aiI                      π(b̃Ii ) = bIi                   π(t̃ij ) = tij              (5.4)

and is an isomorphism in a-degrees zero and one.

                                                         62
7     Completing the proof of flatness, of Theorem 1,
      and stage-setting of modularity
To complete the proof that the extended connection J of (4.4) is flat, it remains to prove
the relation δvv Lww − δww Lvv = 0 of (4.19b) and the relation [Lvv , Lww ] = 0 of (4.20b).
These results will be established in the remainder of this section.


7.1     Proving the flatness condition δvv Lww − δww Lvv = 0
Lemma 7.1. We have the flatness condition,

                                δvv [Lww , X] − δww [Lvv , X] = 0                              (7.1)

Proof. To prove this flatness condition means proving that the condition is realized when
applied to an arbitrary (moduli-independent) element X ∈ t̂h,n . Given that Lww was
established to be a derivation of t̂h,n in Theorem 6.7, it suffices to prove this condition
on the elementary generators X ∈ {aiI , bIi , tij } with I ∈ {1, · · · , h} and i, j ∈ {1, · · · , n}
which in turn was accomplished in Schemata 4.15.

                                         [Lvv , Lww ] = 0                                      (7.2)

                                                    64
7.3    Summary and completing the proof of Theorem 1
Combining the results obtained in Proposition 4.4, Theorem 4.16, Theorem 6.9 with the
results obtained earlier in this section, we obtain the following summary.

Theorem 7.3. There exists a unique der(t̂h,n )-valued solution Lww = L0ww + L1ww to the
local flatness conditions stated in (4.19), (4.20), (4.21) and (4.23), where L1ww is given
in (4.25) and (4.26) while the action of L0ww on the generators of t̂h,n is given by (4.49).
Moreover, Lww is unique under the assumption that its a-degree is one, in the sense
detailed in Schemata 4.1.
   The construction of the components Lα of L from Lww via (4.18) requires choosing
a non-canonical section s : Th → CS(Σ), as already explained in the introduction, and
made explicit in sections 4.1 and 4.2 in terms of Beltrami differentials. The fact that the
construction obeys the formulation of the local (1, 0) extension problem in section 1.3.1 is
the content of the following theorem.

Theorem 7.4. For any τ 0 ∈ Th , there exists a neighborhood Uτ 0 of τ 0 in which problem
(b′′ )-(c′′ ) in section 1.3.1 admits a solution; i.e., for any section s : Uτ 0 → CS(Σ), there
exists Ls as in (b′′ ) and satisfying (c′′ ).

Proof. The starting observation is that it suffices to construct Ls0 satisfying the conditions
of (c′′ ) for a particular section s0 , as Ls for any other section s is derived from it using
the constraints of (b′′ ). The construction of Ls0 was accomplished in section 4 to section
7.2 and will now be summarized.
    Actually, Ls0 is not constructed all at once on the whole of the product of Cf n (Σ) by a
neighborhood of τ 0 . Rather, we first select a complex structure J0 above τ 0 , and cover Σn
by open subsets U1 × · · · × Un attached to a finite collection of n-tuples U = (U1 , · · · , Un )
of open subsets of Σ, each U being such that the complement of ∪i Ui has a nonempty
interior in Σ. By restriction to Cf n (Σ), the finite collection of n-tuples U induces a finite
cover of Cf n (Σ) (section 4.1).
    We then attach to each U a section sU , which is defined on a neighborhood Uτ 0 ,U of
τ 0 such that τ 0 7→ J0 , and which satisfies the vanishing condition (4.3) on the associated
Beltrami differentials (see section 4.2). We recall that the spaces BDsU ,τ of these Beltrami
differentials, indexed by the elements τ of Uτ 0 ,U , are the images of the differential map of
sU at the various points τ ∈ Uτ 0 ,U . Specifically, the condition satisfied by sU is that any
Beltrami differential in any of these various spaces BDsU ,τ vanishes on ∪i Ui .
     We show that the expression in terms of local coordinates (x|τ ) on (U1 × · · · × Un ) ×
Uτ 0 ,U of the system of partial differential equations bearing on LsU is particularly simple
(sections 4.3 and 4.4). From section 4.5 to the end of section 5, we partially solve the

                                               65
analogue of this system relative to a Lie algebra ûh,n equipped with a morphism ûh,n → t̂h,n
obtained by relaxing some relations of the Lie algebra th,n (see Definition 5.1), leading to
L̃sU (x|τ ) such that the Λ2 (TC∗ Cf n (Σ)) and TC∗ Cf n (Σ) ⊗ T ∗(1,0) Th parts of the system
                                sU      sU                    sU
          (dx + ∂τ )L̃sU + ∂τ J˜DHS − J˜DHS ∧ L̃sU − L̃sU ∧ J˜DHS − L̃sU ∧ L̃sU = 0          (7.12)

which is equivalent to

                               (dx + ∂τ ) J˜sU − J˜sU ∧ J˜sU = 0                             (7.13)

under
                                             sU
                             J˜sU (x|τ ) = J˜DHS (x|τ ) + L̃sU (x|τ )                        (7.14)

are satisfied modulo terms in the ideal I = ker(ûh,n → t̂h,n ) (see (5.2)). Solutions
J˜sU (x|τ ) and J˜sU ′ (x′ |τ ) corresponding to tuples U , U ′ are shown to correspond to
one another through the diffeomorphism (ϕU ,U ′ )n , where ϕU ,U ′ is the transition map
Uτ 0 ,U ∩ Uτ 0 ,U ′ → Diff 0 (Σ) from sU to sU ′ , and therefore glue to a well-defined J˜sU 0 , U 0
being a particular n-tuple. However, since the various open sets are slightly displaced by
applying the diffeomorphisms, the intersection over all tuples U of open sets Uτ 0 ,U must be
further restricted to a smaller neighborhood Uτ 0 of τ 0 in order to ensure that the vertical
trace of the union over all tuples U of the displaced open sets (ϕU 0 ,U )n (U1 ×· · ·×Un ×Uτ 0 )
still covers Σn . Arguments relative to the global behavior of J˜sU 0 on Σ are then applied
to show that it descends to a derivation of t̂h,n (section 6), therefore producing a 1-form on
J sU 0 on Cf n (Σ) × Uτ 0 valued in der(t̂h,n ), which by the above satisfies the Λ2 (T ∗ Cf n (Σ))C
and (T ∗ Cf n (Σ))C ⊗ T ∗(0,1) Th parts of the flatness condition

                              (dx + ∂τ ) J sU 0 − J sU 0 ∧ J sU 0 = 0                        (7.15)

In sections 7.1 and 7.2, we show that J sU 0 also satisfies the Λ2 T ∗(0,1) Th part of this con-
dition, and therefore is a solution of (c′′ ) relative to sU 0 = s0 . This ends the construction
of the announced Ls0 and therefore the proof of the theorem.


7.4     Modular properties of Lww
This section is dedicated to establishing the transformation properties of Lww (x|τ ) under
the modular group Sp(2h, Z), whose action on the homology was defined in (2.28). Key
ingredients are the modular tensor properties of the DHS kernels, A and D given by
Lemma 2.5, and the modular transformations of the generators of th,n given in Proposition
2.6. Combined, they lead to the modular invariance of the DHS connection as reviewed
in section 2.5 and together with Theorem 6.7 imply the following result for Lww .

                                                66
Proposition 7.5. In the decomposition (4.24) of Lww (x|τ ), the t̂h,n -valued component
                                                                                 1
L1ww (x|τ ) is modular invariant (as are both of its components Vww (x|τ ) and Vww (τ ) given
                                    0
in (4.26)) while the action of Lww (τ ) ∈ der(t̂h,n ) determined by Theorem 4.16 is modular
covariant, provided that aiI , bIi , tij transform under Sp(2h, Z) as in (2.33).
                                                                                       1
Proof. Modular invariance of the x-dependent and t̂h,n -valued parts Vww (x|τ ) + Vww     (τ )
of Lww (x|τ ) is manifest term-by-term from their expressions in (4.26): the tensorial
Sp(2h, Z) transformation of DHS kernels in (2.32) and of aiI , bIi , tij in (2.33) imply that
the generating series θG(w, xi ; Bi ) and ΦJ (w; Bk )akJ in (4.26a) are individually modular
invariant and that the (2, 0)w forms ω J (w)∂w ΦJ I⃗K (w) in (4.26b) compensate the modular
                                                        ⃗
transformation of the accompanying t̂h,n elements BkI akK .
     It remains to show that, for arbitrary X ∈ t̂h,n , the der(t̂h,n ) action [L0ww (τ ), X] trans-
forms under Sp(2h, Z) in the same way as X does by (2.33). At the level of the generators
aiI , bIi , tij of t̂h,n , this is the case for each term in the expressions (4.49) for their brackets
with L0ww (τ ): by Lemma 2.5 together with (4.50) and (4.51), the Sp(2h, Z) transformation
of the modular tensors δww AM P⃗ N and δww DP⃗ in (4.49) is inverse to that of the accompa-
nying t̂h,n elements, up to the uncontracted lower index I in the first line and the upper
                                                                                         ⃗       ⃗
index I in the second line. By these uncontracted indices, δww AM θ(P⃗ )I Q⃗ N [BiP aiM , BiQ aiN ]
                      ⃗       ⃗
and δww Dθ(P⃗ )I Q⃗ [BiP tik , BiQ tik ] in the first line of (4.49) transform like aiI of [L0ww , aiI ] on
                                  ⃗
the left side and δww AI P⃗ J BiP aiJ in the second line transforms like bIi of [L0ww , bIi ] on the left
                                        ⃗
side. The contributions δww DP⃗ [BiP tij , tij ] to the right side of the third line are modular
invariant just like the tij of [L0ww , tij ] on the left side.
    We have demonstrated that [L0ww (τ ), X] and X exhibit the same modular transforma-
tion for all generators X ∈ {aiI , bIi , tij } of t̂h,n with I ∈ {1, · · · , h} and i, j ∈ {1, · · · , n}.
Given that Lww (x|τ ) is a derivation of t̂h,n by Theorem 6.7, the same is true for arbitrary
X ∈ t̂h,n , and we conclude that L0ww (τ ) enjoys a modular covariant action on t̂h,n .
    In order to infer from Proposition 7.5, expressing the modularity of Lww , a modularity
result on the (1, 0)-form components Lα in holomorphic moduli directions via (4.18), it
remains to combine this result with the Sp(2h, Z) properties of the Beltrami differentials,
also see footnote 10 (section 1.5). The leftover work to deduce modularity of L = Ls is
relegated to future work.




                                                    67
8     Relations between A, D, N and flatness conditions
                             (1,0)   (1,0)
The flatness conditions [Ji , Jj ] = 0 for the DHS connection JDHS given in (2.22a)
were shown in [30] to be equivalent to the set of all the interchange and Fay identities on
DHS kernels that had earlier been derived by independent methods in [31]. In this sense,
                         (1,0) (1,0)
the flatness relations [Ji , Jj ] = 0 are generating functions for all interchange and Fay
identities. Both the flatness relations and the interchange and Fay identities play a crucial
role in making the algebra of DHS kernels together with the associated polylogarithms
closed under taking primitives.
    Having extended the DHS connection JDHS on a fixed Riemann surface Σ to a flat
connection J that includes holomorphic variations in the moduli of Σ, we dispose of new
flatness conditions as well as of the relations ensuring that L0ww belongs to the derivation
algebra der(t̂h,n ). The novel protagonist in all these relations is L0ww determined by its
action on the generators aiI , bIi and tij given in Theorem 4.16 in terms of the variational
derivatives of the modular tensors A and D of (2.10). Therefore, the flatness conditions
of the connection J and the conditions for L0ww ∈ der(t̂h,n ) require relations between A
and D. The new relations arise in two categories.
    1. Linear relations between A and D, such as those of Proposition 2.2 which played,
       for example, a crucial role in the proof of Theorem 5.3 given in appendix G;
    2. Bilinear relations between the variational derivatives of A and D and DHS kernels
       that arise from the flatness condition [Lvv , Lww ] = 0.
Investigations into the relations of item 2. are relegated to future work. In the remainder
of this section, we provide additional discussion of the linear relations of item 1.

8.1     Linear relations
