8.2     Bilinear relations
The flatness condition [Lvv , Lww ] = 0 may be expressed in terms of its vanishing action on
the generators a, b and t of t̂h,n . The resulting relations are all bilinear in the variational
derivatives of A and D. These variational derivatives may be expressed in terms of DHS
kernels ∂Φ and the modular tensors N with the help of (3.42) and (3.43). The nature
and structure of the induced relations constitutes an open problem analogous in spirit to
the Fay identities between DHS kernels.

                                                   69
9      Restriction to genus one
In this section, we shall discuss the special case of genus h = 1 for which many sim-
plifications take place. In addition, this will give us the opportunity to compare the
formal structure and concrete formulas of the single-valued and modular invariant, but
non-meromorphic, flat connection of this paper with the meromorphic global connection
of [12]. In particular, we shall be able to identify Tsunogai’s derivations [32] as part of
the algebra where the connection J at h = 1 and that of [12] takes values.
    We shall represent a genus one Riemann surface Σ as the quotient Σ = C/(Z + τ Z)
where the modulus τ takes values in the upper half plane, parametrized by τ = τ1 + iτ2
with τ1 , τ2 ∈ R and τ2 > 0. The customary choice of complex coordinates z, z̄ on the torus
implements the quotient representation by identifying z ∼= z +1 along a homology A1 cycle
and z ∼ = z + τ along a B1 cycle so that the normalized holomorphic Abelian differential
is ω1 = dz. As a result, the coordinates z, z̄ and the differential dz are τ -dependent.
The choice of complex coordinates x, x̄ adopted in the preceding sections of this paper
is different from the customary choice in that x, x̄ are independent of the moduli. The
change of variables x → z(x) between the two choices is easily realized in terms of the
differential ω1 = dz = ω1 (x)dx with ω1 (x) = ∂z/∂x.

relations of Definition 2.3 and the following echo of translation invariance on the torus,

which closely resembles the z-dependence of the analogous dτ component of the CEE
connection [12]: the latter is formally obtained from the single-valued and modular L1 (z|τ )
in (9.33) by converting f (r+2) → g (r+2) to the meromorphic Kronecker-Eisenstein kernels.
This formal relation via f (r+2) → g (r+2) signals that our single-valued and modular global
flat connection (9.7) is obtained from a gauge transformation of the meromorphic CEE
connection. In the case of a single variable zi = ui + τ vi on the torus, the replacement
f (r+2) → g (r+2) is attained through a gauge transformation with logarithm ∼ ui bi , see
section 2.3 of [9] for the case of the Brown-Levin connection and section 5.1 of [13] for its
extension to the moduli space M1,2 . In our setting of multiple variables zi on the torus
with i ∈ {1, · · · , n}, the analogous gauge transformation between the meromorphic CEE
connection and the single-valued and modular extended flat connection (9.7) is given by
the exponential of ni=1 ui bi .

A.2      Lie algebra identities
The following Lie algebra relation was originally stated and proven in Lemma E.2 of [30],
          I⃗                        X X h ⃗ Q⃗                  ⃗  θ(Y

                            ⃗      ⃗                      ⃗ ⃗
                                                 X

                                                                       

Proof. The proof is by induction on the length s of the word J⃗ = J1 · · · Js . The statement
clearly holds when s = 0 since there are then no contributions to the second term on the
right. For s ≥ 1 we peel off one factor of Bj followed by BjJ aiI = δIJ tij ,

           aiI , BjJ1 · · · BjJs X = BjJ1 aiI , BjJ2 · · · BjJs X + δIJ1 BjJ2 · · · BjJs X, tij
                                                                                           
                                                                                                  (A.7)

and then use the validity of the relation at orders ≤ s − 1 to evaluate the first term.

B      Coincident limits of the Fay identities
In this appendix, we construct the one coincident limit of the Fay identities involving three
points x, y, z ∈ Σ that was not constructed in [31], namely from the (1, 0)x ⊗(1, 0)y ⊗(0, 0)z
form of the Fay identities to a (2, 0)w ⊗ (0, 0)z form when x, y → w. The starting point
is the general Fay identity in Theorem 6.2 of [31] where the product of two DHS kernels
with a common scalar point z is expressed in terms of a sum of binary products where at
most one factor is z-dependent. For the problem at hand, it will suffice to take the trace
in the indices K and M of this identity, whose decomposition into ∂Φ and ∂G may be
recast as follows after some simplifications, 39

D      Proof of Proposition 3.2
In this appendix, we prove Proposition 3.2 which gives the variational derivative of the
Arakelov Green function in equations (3.37) and (3.38). The starting point for the proof
is the explicit expression for the Arakelov Green function G(x, y) in terms of Abelian
integrals and the prime form obtained in [18]. The construction uses the “string Green
function” G(x, y) which, like the Abelian integrals and the prime form, is not single-valued
on Σ but rather should be defined on a given fundamental domain D for Σ, as follows,
                                                  Z x            Z x 
                                       2                    IJ
               G(x, y) = − ln |E(x, y)| + 2π Im       ωI Y       Im    ωJ              (D.1)
                                                       y                  y

The relation is as follows,
                            G(x, y) = G(x, y) − γ(x) − γ(y) + γ0                             (D.2)
where γ(x) and γ0 are given in terms of the Kähler form κ(z) of (2.2) by,
                   Z                                       Z
                        2
            γ(x) =     d z κ(z) G(x, z)               γ0 =   d2 z κ(z) γ(z)                  (D.3)
                        D                                         D
While G(x, y), γ(x) and γ0 depend on the choice of the fundamental domain D, the
combination G(x, y) is single-valued in x, y ∈ Σ and independent of the choice of D.
    Using the variational derivative for the prime form derived in [53],
                                        1                                 2
                  δww ln E(x, y) = − ∂w ln E(w, x) − ∂w ln E(w, y)                            (D.4)
                                        2
and the formulas for the variational derivatives of ωI , ω̄I and Y given in (3.35), one readily
evaluates the variational derivative of G(x, y), and we obtain,
                                         1                            2
                         δww G(x, y) =      ∂w G(w, x) − ∂w G(w, y)
                                         2
                                         1                            2
                                      =     ∂w G(w, x) − ∂w G(w, y)                           (D.5)
                                         2
where we passed from the first line to the second using (D.2). The variational derivatives of
the combinations γ(x) and γ0 may then be evaluated using (3.36) and the above expression
for δww G(x, y) expressed in terms of the Arakelov Green function on the second line of
(D.5), and we obtain,
                                          Z
                   1            2 1                              2
       δww γ(x) = ∂w G(w, x) +               d2 z κ(z) ∂w G(w, z)
                   2                   2 Σ
                              Z
                     1                                                     
                  − ω I (w)      d2 z ∂z ∂w G(w, z) ω̄I (z) G(z, x) + γ(z)
                     h         D
                  Z                                        Z
                       2
                                            2 2 I
         δww γ0 =     d z κ(z) ∂w G(w, z) − ω (w)             d2 z ∂z ∂w G(w, z) ω̄I (z) γ(z) (D.6)
                    Σ                              h        D


                                                83
Combining these contributions with the help of (D.2), all D-dependent integrals, the
squares of ∂w G(w, ·) and their integrals over Σ against κ(z) manifestly cancel one another,
such that we obtain the following formula,
                                                      Z
                                               1 I
     δww G(x, y) = −∂w G(w, x) ∂w G(w, y) + ω (w) d2 z ∂z ∂w G(w, z) ω̄I (z) G(z, x)
                                               h        Σ
                               Z
                       1 I
                    + ω (w) d2 z ∂z ∂w G(w, z) ω̄I (z) G(z, y)                        (D.7)
                       h        Σ

Since G and the Abelian differentials are single-valued on Σ, we may integrate by parts
in z and express the result in terms of the DHS kernel GI defined in the first line of (2.4)
to obtain (3.37) and (3.38) and thereby proving Proposition 3.2.

E      Proof of Proposition 3.3
The proposition is proven by direct calculation of the variational derivatives of the defining
formulas in (2.10) using the variational derivatives of the primitives of DHS kernels in
(3.41) and the defining relations for their coincident limits CI⃗(w) in (B.2). For the modular
tensors A, the proof of the variational derivative (3.42) is straightforward. For the modular
tensors DI⃗, we use their definition in (2.10) and the variational derivatives of κ(x) and

and its i → j counterpart as well as the structure relation [tik + tjk , tij ] = 0. We are
left with (F.24), which involves only the variables w and x. To prove that (F.24) is

            n                      o                                        

The proof that J˜ij belongs to I may be completed by contradiction. We use the fact that
  (1,0)
J˜ij admits a Taylor expansion in powers of b̃, each term having positive b̃-degree. We
denote the term of lowest b̃-degree that does not belong to I by X. Then, by equation
(H.9), X must be holomorphic in xi , if it exists, up to terms that belong to I, since
the right side of (H.9) has b̃-degree at least one higher than X and is therefore a linear
combination of the holomorphic Abelian differentials ω̄ L (xi ) up to terms that belong to I.
But the integral of this non-vanishing lowest b̃-degree term against ω̄ K (xi ) must belong
                                                                                         (1,0)
to I, as shown in (H.5), thereby contradicting our initial assumption. As a result J˜ij

                  (1,0)
     Given that J˜ij    ∈ I is preserved by its variational derivative, we have established
